    "newString": "Catastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.\nWhen a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nWithout machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth improve extraction quality \\emph{and} produce evidence annotations.\nHowever, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.\nIn SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.\nThe evidence signal \\emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nEvidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.\nWhether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.\nThis uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning four model scales (1.7B to 8B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.\n\\textbf{First}, we identify a \\emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.\nGRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.\nQLoRA acts as a catastrophic amplifier of the underlying optimization conflict rather than its root cause.\n\\textbf{Second}, we discover a \\emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.\nAt 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +8 percentage points (pp) in Ign-F1; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at 8B, the tax approaches zero.\nThis pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with Natural Language Inference (NLI) based Contrastive Evidence Discrimination (CED) scoring emerges as the only effective optimization route.\nBy selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that quantifies the degree of misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, capturing this relationship with method-level $\\rho = -0.965$.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: a scale-varying cost pattern across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters that challenges the assumption that evidence production uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based CED scoring} as an effective solution, achieving +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean). Our 1.7B RSFT model (50.50 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

### Tool result

_source line 3324 · 2026-05-18T07:09:04.943Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015QnuqsMndbpgEYGvX2FAib`</summary>

```text
49	Different RL algorithms produce qualitatively different failure modes when applied to joint evidence-and-extraction generation. We organize these outcomes along a \emph{decoupling spectrum} ranging from catastrophic collapse to mild perturbation, shown in Table~\ref{tab:spectrum} (visualized in Figure~\ref{fig:spectrum}, Appendix).
50	
51	\paragraph{GRPO QLoRA: catastrophic collapse.}
52	Under QLoRA quantization ($G{=}4$), GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 0.0138 (near zero extraction) while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 and EDCR reaches 0.9876. The model learns to produce well-formed evidence sentences but loses the ability to predict correct relation triples. This extreme decoupling occurs because 4-bit quantization constrains the effective parameter space, forcing the reward signal to optimize whichever sub-task yields easier gradient progress. Evidence sentence generation, being closer to extractive summarization, wins this competition.
53	
54	\paragraph{GRPO bf16: mild, variable.}
55	Full-precision GRPO ($G{=}8$) avoids catastrophe. Its training trajectory shows non-monotonic Rel-F1 fluctuation (0.434 at step 30, 0.428 at step 50, 0.434 at step 70, 0.455 at step 100), stabilizing near the SFT baseline. The best checkpoint achieves Rel-F1 0.4549 with Evi-F1 80.86 and EDCR 0.6318. The decoupling is mild: bf16 precision preserves enough capacity for both sub-tasks to improve jointly, though the variable trajectory suggests fragile optimization dynamics that require careful checkpoint selection.
56	
```

          "+Under QLoRA quantization ($G{=}4$), GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 0.0138 (near zero extraction) while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 and EDCR reaches 0.9876. {
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
  "old_string": "Our contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that quantifies the degree of misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, capturing this relationship with method-level $\\rho = -0.965$.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: a scale-varying cost pattern across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters that challenges the assumption that evidence production uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based CED scoring} as an effective solution, achieving +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean). Our 1.7B RSFT model (50.50 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}",
  "new_string": "Our contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that tracks misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, as an analytical tool throughout our study. EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction; we interpret the observed correlation ($\\rho = -0.965$) as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent finding, and highlight its per-relation diagnostic value for identifying which relation types suffer from extraction-evidence misalignment.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of joint evidence production varies across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters, challenging the assumption that evidence generation uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective solution, using CED or flat-NLI to select high-quality training data. RSFT achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (50.50 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}"
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.\nWhen a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nWithout machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth improve extraction quality \\emph{and} produce evidence annotations.\nHowever, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.\nIn SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.\nThe evidence signal \\emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nEvidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.\nWhether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.\nThis uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning four model scales (1.7B to 8B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.\n\\textbf{First}, we identify a \\emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.\nGRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.\nCatastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.\n\\textbf{Second}, we discover a \\emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.\nAt 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +8 percentage points (pp) in Ign-F1; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at 8B, the tax approaches zero.\nThis pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with Natural Language Inference (NLI) based Contrastive Evidence Discrimination (CED) scoring emerges as the only effective optimization route.\nBy selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that quantifies the degree of misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, capturing this relationship with method-level $\\rho = -0.965$.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: a scale-varying cost pattern across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters that challenges the assumption that evidence production uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based CED scoring} as an effective solution, achieving +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean). Our 1.7B RSFT model (50.50 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

{

    "oldString": "\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with Natural Language Inference (NLI) based Contrastive Evidence Discrimination (CED) scoring emerges as the only effective optimization route.",
    "newString": "\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring for training data selection emerges as the most effective optimization route.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.\nWhen a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nWithout machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth improve extraction quality \\emph{and} produce evidence annotations.\nHowever, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.\nIn SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.\nThe evidence signal \\emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nEvidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.\nWhether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.\nThis uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning four model scales (1.7B to 8B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.\n\\textbf{First}, we identify a \\emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.\nGRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.\nCatastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.\n\\textbf{Second}, we discover a \\emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.\nAt 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +8 percentage points (pp) in Ign-F1; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at 8B, the tax approaches zero.\nThis pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with Natural Language Inference (NLI) based Contrastive Evidence Discrimination (CED) scoring emerges as the only effective optimization route.\nBy selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that tracks misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, as an analytical tool throughout our study. EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction; we interpret the observed correlation ($\\rho = -0.965$) as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent finding, and highlight its per-relation diagnostic value for identifying which relation types suffer from extraction-evidence misalignment.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of joint evidence production varies across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters, challenging the assumption that evidence generation uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective solution, using CED or flat-NLI to select high-quality training data. RSFT achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (50.50 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

We adopt CED as the default because its per-triple discrimination scores support interpretable error analysis.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Task: Evidence-Grounded Document-Level Relation Extraction}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a document $D = \\{s_1, \\ldots, s_n\\}$ consisting of $n$ sentences and a set of entities $\\mathcal{E}$ identified within $D$, the task of evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires predicting, for each entity pair $(e_h, e_t) \\in \\mathcal{E} \\times \\mathcal{E}$, a relation $r \\in \\mathcal{R} \\cup \\{\\texttt{NA}\\}$ together with a set of supporting evidence sentences $E \\subseteq D$. Standard DocRE predicts only the triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$; the evidence-grounded formulation also requires $E$, which grounds each prediction in specific textual support.\n\nIn our LLM-based approach, the model generates structured outputs that jointly encode both components. For each predicted relation, the output follows the format \\texttt{(head, relation, tail) | Evidence: [S$i$][S$j$]}, requiring the model to produce complete evidence references inline with each triple rather than as a separate retrieval step. This design contrasts with encoder-based evidence methods such as SAIS~\\citep{xiao2022sais} and DREEAM~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}, which frame evidence identification as sentence-level binary classification. Because classification over a fixed sentence set does not increase output length, these methods incur no generation overhead. Our LLM formulation requires additional tokens for each evidence reference, creating a generation burden that scales with the number of predicted triples and their evidence sentences. This scale-dependent cost is the central phenomenon we investigate.\n\n\n\\subsection{Supervised Fine-Tuning Baseline}\n\\label{sec:sft}\n\nWe fine-tune all models with LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} at rank $r{=}64$ and scaling factor $\\alpha{=}128$, applied to attention projection matrices. The training data consists of DocRED~\\citep{yao2019docred} training documents converted to the structured output format described above, where each example maps a document and its entity set to the complete set of relation triples with evidence annotations. We also train a \\emph{no-evidence} variant under identical configuration but with evidence references removed from the output format, producing only \\texttt{(head, relation, tail)}. This variant serves as an upper bound on extraction performance by eliminating the generation cost of evidence, and allows direct measurement of the evidence tax at each model scale. Detailed hyperparameters (learning rate, batch size, number of epochs) are reported in the Appendix.\n\n\n\\subsection{Optimization Paradigms}\n\\label{sec:paradigms}\n\nWe evaluate three post-SFT optimization paradigms that differ in how they incorporate quality signals from the CED scoring function (\\S\\ref{sec:ced}).\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO.}\nGroup Relative Policy Optimization~\\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA with $G{=}8$. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}.\n\n\\paragraph{DPO.}\nDirect Preference Optimization~\\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as a classification problem over preference pairs, without explicit reward models. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. DPO then adjusts the policy to increase the log-probability ratio between preferred and rejected completions. We train for 1 and 3 epochs to observe the temporal dynamics of evidence-extraction trade-offs under implicit optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{RSFT.}\nRejection Sampling Fine-Tuning is a single-round variant of iterative self-improvement methods such as STaR~\\citep{zelikman2022star}. The procedure is straightforward: the SFT model generates $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per training document, each candidate is scored by CED, and only the top-$k{=}1$ response is retained as training data. From 24,216 candidate outputs, this selection yields 9,042 training examples (37.3\\% acceptance rate). Unlike GRPO and DPO, RSFT does not apply gradient-based optimization pressure toward a reward signal. Instead, it improves training data quality through filtering: the model trains on its own best outputs rather than all outputs. This distinction is critical because discrete selection over complete outputs cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find. The contribution of RSFT in our setting is not the framework itself, which is well-established, but its application to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED as a domain-specific quality filter.\n\n\n\\subsection{Contrastive Evidence Discrimination}\n\\label{sec:ced}\n\nCED is a scoring function that evaluates the faithfulness of predicted evidence sentences to their associated relation triples. It builds on Natural Language Inference (NLI) to measure whether evidence actually entails the predicted relation, rather than merely co-occurring with the relevant entities.\n\nFor a predicted triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$ with evidence sentences $E$, CED constructs a positive NLI pair: the premise is the concatenation of sentences in $E$, and the hypothesis is a natural-language verbalization of the relation (e.g., ``$e_h$ has relation $r$ with $e_t$''). An NLI model (\\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}) produces an entailment probability $p^+$ for this pair. The contrastive component introduces hard negatives drawn from the same document. For each triple, we collect sentences that mention $e_h$ or $e_t$ but are not in the predicted evidence set. These entity-sharing distractors serve as negative premises, and the NLI model scores each to obtain $p^-_1, \\ldots, p^-_m$. The CED score for a single triple is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\text{CED}(e_h, r, e_t, E) = p^+ - \\max_j \\, p^-_j\n\\label{eq:ced}\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation forces the scoring function to distinguish sentences that genuinely support the relation from sentences that merely mention the same entities. A high CED score indicates that the evidence provides relation-specific support beyond entity co-occurrence.\n\nThe document-level CED score aggregates over all predicted triples, weighted by extraction correctness: triples that match ground-truth relations contribute their CED scores, while incorrect triples receive zero credit. This coupling ensures that CED rewards both faithful evidence and accurate extraction jointly.\n\nWe also evaluate a \\emph{flat-NLI} baseline that uses only the positive entailment score $p^+$ without the contrastive term. In our experiments (Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}), CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (mean Rel-F1 difference of 0.02pp across available seeds), and both outperform random selection and no-scoring baselines. We adopt CED as the default because the contrastive formulation provides interpretable per-triple discrimination scores that flat-NLI lacks.\n\n\n\\subsection{Evidence-Decoupling Ratio}\n\\label{sec:edcr}\n\nWe introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric that quantifies the degree of misalignment between a model's extraction and evidence capabilities. EDCR is not a training component; it serves purely as an analytical tool for comparing optimization methods.\n\nEDCR is defined as:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\text{EDCR} = \\frac{\\text{Evi-Recall}_{\\text{TP}}}{\\text{Rel-Recall}}\n\\label{eq:edcr}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\text{Evi-Recall}_{\\text{TP}}$ is evidence sentence recall computed only over correctly predicted triples (true positives), and $\\text{Rel-Recall}$ is overall relation extraction recall. The numerator captures evidence quality on the subset of triples the model gets right; the denominator captures how many triples the model identifies in total.\n\nThe intuition is as follows. A model that correctly identifies a few triples and provides excellent evidence for them, but misses most triples entirely, will have high $\\text{Evi-Recall}_{\\text{TP}}$ and low $\\text{Rel-Recall}$, yielding a high EDCR. This pattern signals that evidence quality has decoupled from extraction capability: the model produces faithful evidence only within a narrow comfort zone.\n\nEDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across optimization paradigms reveals a strong negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.965$): methods with higher EDCR produce worse extraction, confirming that elevated EDCR signals problematic decoupling. At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals variation that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\\rho{=}{-}0.064$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6982) reflects the inherent cost of evidence generation; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6503) reflect improved co-optimization.\n",

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We formalize evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction as a structured generation task for LLMs and compare three post-training paradigms across 85+ configurations. RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic, DPO gradual, RSFT none; EDCR $\rho = -0.965$), and the cost of joint evidence production varies across scales (+8pp at 1.7B, $-$7.5pp at 4B, near zero at 8B). RSFT with CED scoring yields +4.92pp Ign-F1 at 4B (four-seed mean); our 1.7B RSFT model (50.50 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT (50.51) at a fraction of the cost.
5	
```

    "newString": "At the method level, comparing EDCR across optimization paradigms reveals a negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.965$). Because EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction, this correlation should be interpreted as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent discovery; its primary value lies in per-relation analysis (below).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Task: Evidence-Grounded Document-Level Relation Extraction}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a document $D = \\{s_1, \\ldots, s_n\\}$ consisting of $n$ sentences and a set of entities $\\mathcal{E}$ identified within $D$, the task of evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires predicting, for each entity pair $(e_h, e_t) \\in \\mathcal{E} \\times \\mathcal{E}$, a relation $r \\in \\mathcal{R} \\cup \\{\\texttt{NA}\\}$ together with a set of supporting evidence sentences $E \\subseteq D$. Standard DocRE predicts only the triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$; the evidence-grounded formulation also requires $E$, which grounds each prediction in specific textual support.\n\nIn our LLM-based approach, the model generates structured outputs that jointly encode both components. For each predicted relation, the output follows the format \\texttt{(head, relation, tail) | Evidence: [S$i$][S$j$]}, requiring the model to produce complete evidence references inline with each triple rather than as a separate retrieval step. This design contrasts with encoder-based evidence methods such as SAIS~\\citep{xiao2022sais} and DREEAM~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}, which frame evidence identification as sentence-level binary classification. Because classification over a fixed sentence set does not increase output length, these methods incur no generation overhead. Our LLM formulation requires additional tokens for each evidence reference, creating a generation burden that scales with the number of predicted triples and their evidence sentences. This scale-dependent cost is the central phenomenon we investigate.\n\n\n\\subsection{Supervised Fine-Tuning Baseline}\n\\label{sec:sft}\n\nWe fine-tune all models with LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} at rank $r{=}64$ and scaling factor $\\alpha{=}128$, applied to attention projection matrices. The training data consists of DocRED~\\citep{yao2019docred} training documents converted to the structured output format described above, where each example maps a document and its entity set to the complete set of relation triples with evidence annotations. We also train a \\emph{no-evidence} variant under identical configuration but with evidence references removed from the output format, producing only \\texttt{(head, relation, tail)}. This variant serves as an upper bound on extraction performance by eliminating the generation cost of evidence, and allows direct measurement of the evidence tax at each model scale. Detailed hyperparameters (learning rate, batch size, number of epochs) are reported in the Appendix.\n\n\n\\subsection{Optimization Paradigms}\n\\label{sec:paradigms}\n\nWe evaluate three post-SFT optimization paradigms that differ in how they incorporate quality signals from the CED scoring function (\\S\\ref{sec:ced}).\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO.}\nGroup Relative Policy Optimization~\\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA with $G{=}8$. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}.\n\n\\paragraph{DPO.}\nDirect Preference Optimization~\\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as a classification problem over preference pairs, without explicit reward models. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. DPO then adjusts the policy to increase the log-probability ratio between preferred and rejected completions. We train for 1 and 3 epochs to observe the temporal dynamics of evidence-extraction trade-offs under implicit optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{RSFT.}\nRejection Sampling Fine-Tuning is a single-round variant of iterative self-improvement methods such as STaR~\\citep{zelikman2022star}. The procedure is straightforward: the SFT model generates $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per training document, each candidate is scored by CED, and only the top-$k{=}1$ response is retained as training data. From 24,216 candidate outputs, this selection yields 9,042 training examples (37.3\\% acceptance rate). Unlike GRPO and DPO, RSFT does not apply gradient-based optimization pressure toward a reward signal. Instead, it improves training data quality through filtering: the model trains on its own best outputs rather than all outputs. This distinction is critical because discrete selection over complete outputs cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find. The contribution of RSFT in our setting is not the framework itself, which is well-established, but its application to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED as a domain-specific quality filter.\n\n\n\\subsection{Contrastive Evidence Discrimination}\n\\label{sec:ced}\n\nCED is a scoring function that evaluates the faithfulness of predicted evidence sentences to their associated relation triples. It builds on Natural Language Inference (NLI) to measure whether evidence actually entails the predicted relation, rather than merely co-occurring with the relevant entities.\n\nFor a predicted triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$ with evidence sentences $E$, CED constructs a positive NLI pair: the premise is the concatenation of sentences in $E$, and the hypothesis is a natural-language verbalization of the relation (e.g., ``$e_h$ has relation $r$ with $e_t$''). An NLI model (\\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}) produces an entailment probability $p^+$ for this pair. The contrastive component introduces hard negatives drawn from the same document. For each triple, we collect sentences that mention $e_h$ or $e_t$ but are not in the predicted evidence set. These entity-sharing distractors serve as negative premises, and the NLI model scores each to obtain $p^-_1, \\ldots, p^-_m$. The CED score for a single triple is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\text{CED}(e_h, r, e_t, E) = p^+ - \\max_j \\, p^-_j\n\\label{eq:ced}\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation forces the scoring function to distinguish sentences that genuinely support the relation from sentences that merely mention the same entities. A high CED score indicates that the evidence provides relation-specific support beyond entity co-occurrence.\n\nThe document-level CED score aggregates over all predicted triples, weighted by extraction correctness: triples that match ground-truth relations contribute their CED scores, while incorrect triples receive zero credit. This coupling ensures that CED rewards both faithful evidence and accurate extraction jointly.\n\nWe also evaluate a \\emph{flat-NLI} baseline that uses only the positive entailment score $p^+$ without the contrastive term. In our experiments (Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}), CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (mean Rel-F1 difference of 0.02pp across available seeds), and both outperform random selection and no-scoring baselines. Experiments show that CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (Section~\\ref{sec:analysis}), confirming that the contrastive formulation is not essential for data selection quality. We adopt CED as the default because its per-triple discrimination scores support interpretable error analysis.\n\n\n\\subsection{Evidence-Decoupling Ratio}\n\\label{sec:edcr}\n\nWe introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric that quantifies the degree of misalignment between a model's extraction and evidence capabilities. EDCR is not a training component; it serves purely as an analytical tool for comparing optimization methods.\n\nEDCR is defined as:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\text{EDCR} = \\frac{\\text{Evi-Recall}_{\\text{TP}}}{\\text{Rel-Recall}}\n\\label{eq:edcr}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\text{Evi-Recall}_{\\text{TP}}$ is evidence sentence recall computed only over correctly predicted triples (true positives), and $\\text{Rel-Recall}$ is overall relation extraction recall. The numerator captures evidence quality on the subset of triples the model gets right; the denominator captures how many triples the model identifies in total.\n\nThe intuition is as follows. A model that correctly identifies a few triples and provides excellent evidence for them, but misses most triples entirely, will have high $\\text{Evi-Recall}_{\\text{TP}}$ and low $\\text{Rel-Recall}$, yielding a high EDCR. This pattern signals that evidence quality has decoupled from extraction capability: the model produces faithful evidence only within a narrow comfort zone.\n\nEDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across optimization paradigms reveals a strong negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.965$): methods with higher EDCR produce worse extraction, confirming that elevated EDCR signals problematic decoupling. At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals variation that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\\rho{=}{-}0.064$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6982) reflects the inherent cost of evidence generation; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6503) reflect improved co-optimization.\n",

```text
155	\section{Per-Relation EDCR Distribution}
156	\label{sec:edcr_per_relation}
157	
158	Computing EDCR for each of the 76 relation types with $\geq$5 evaluation samples reveals that per-relation EDCR correlates strongly with per-relation Rel-F1 ($\rho = -0.793$, $p < 0.001$), confirming its utility as a difficulty indicator. However, per-relation EDCR is statistically independent of Evi-F1 ($\rho = -0.064$, $p = 0.58$), and Evi-F1 itself shows no association with Rel-F1 ($\rho = -0.013$, $p = 0.91$). These null correlations establish that evidence quality and extraction ability are fully decoupled across relation types. Extreme cases illustrate the diagnostic power: \texttt{date\_of\_birth} has EDCR $= 0.118$ (highly faithful, with extraction and evidence tightly coupled), while \texttt{head\_of\_government} reaches $0.956$ (highly decoupled). Method-level EDCR serves as a horizontal comparison tool across optimization paradigms, while per-relation EDCR enables vertical diagnosis of which relation types suffer from extraction-evidence misalignment.
159	
160	\begin{figure}[t]
161	\centering
162	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig_edcr_relations.pdf}
163	\caption{Per-relation EDCR distribution across 93 relation types. Red bars indicate relations with EDCR $\geq$ 0.70 (severe decoupling), green bars indicate EDCR $<$ 0.30 (healthy evidence grounding). Seven relation types exhibit EDCR = 1.0, indicating complete evidence decoupling.}
164	\label{fig:edcr_relations}
165	\end{figure}
166

```text
1	\section{Limitations}
2	
3	All results use the DocRED development set; test set submission is pending. Exact-match F1 penalizes surface-form-different entity mentions~\citep{wan2024genres}, likely underestimating absolute extraction capability, though relative comparisons remain valid.
4	
5	We evaluate one algorithm per RL family (GRPO, DPO); other algorithms may yield different results. CED scoring depends on an external NLI model, and the RSFT ablation uses limited seeds (four for CED, two for flat-NLI). The evidence tax pattern observed up to 8B may not extrapolate to larger scales. At 1.7B scale, 44.4\% of outputs exhibit repetitive patterns (vs.\ negligible at 4B+); small models may require decoding-time interventions (e.g., repetition penalty) for reliable deployment.
6	
```

{

  "old_string": "We release code and model outputs to support reproducibility.",
  "new_string": "We release code and model outputs to support reproducibility.\n\n\\section*{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}\n\n\\paragraph{A. Did you describe the limitations of your work?}\nYes. Section ``Limitations'' discusses evaluation on development set only, limited RL algorithm coverage, seed count, scale extrapolation, and 1.7B repetition issues.\n\n\\paragraph{B. Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?}\nYes. The Ethics Statement discusses data sources, absence of human subjects, and the intended use for improving verifiability in high-stakes domains.\n\n\\paragraph{C. Do the abstract and introduction summarize the paper's main claims?}\nYes. The abstract states three findings (decoupling spectrum, scale-dependent evidence tax, RSFT effectiveness) with supporting numbers. The introduction provides detailed motivation, findings, and a contribution list.\n\n\\paragraph{D1. Did you include the code, data, and instructions needed to reproduce the main experimental results?}\nWe will release training and evaluation code upon publication. All experiments use publicly available datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED) and base models (Qwen3, LLaMA-3.1) accessible through standard channels.\n\n\\paragraph{D2. Did you specify all the training and test details?}\nYes. Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:hyperparams} provides complete hyperparameters for all methods. Section~\\ref{sec:setup} describes datasets, models, and metrics. We report four seeds for the primary configuration and standard deviations where applicable.\n\n\\paragraph{D3. Did you report error bars or statistical significance?}\nPartially. We report standard deviations for the primary RSFT configuration (four seeds) and per-seed breakdowns in the appendix. Limited seed counts for some configurations (two for flat-NLI, one for 1.7B/8B) are acknowledged as limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{D4. Did you use existing packages for known algorithms?}\nYes. We use HuggingFace Transformers, TRL, and PEFT for model training. The NLI model for CED scoring is a publicly available cross-encoder checkpoint.\n\n\\paragraph{E. Did you use human annotators?}\nNo. All experiments use existing annotated benchmarks.\n\n\\paragraph{F. Did you use AI assistants?}\nYes. AI assistants were used for code development and paper drafting. All scientific claims, experimental design, and analysis are the responsibility of the authors."
}

          "+We will release training and evaluation code upon publication. All experiments use publicly available datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED) and base models (Qwen3, LLaMA-3.1) accessible through standard channels.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{D2. Did you specify all the training and test details?}",
          "+Yes. Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:hyperparams} provides complete hyperparameters for all methods. Section~\\ref{sec:setup} describes datasets, models, and metrics. We report four seeds for the primary configuration and standard deviations where applicable.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{D3. Did you report error bars or statistical significance?}",
          "+Partially. We report standard deviations for the primary RSFT configuration (four seeds) and per-seed breakdowns in the appendix. Limited seed counts for some configurations (two for flat-NLI, one for 1.7B/8B) are acknowledged as limitations.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{D4. Did you use existing packages for known algorithms?}",
          "+Yes. We use HuggingFace Transformers, TRL, and PEFT for model training. The NLI model for CED scoring is a publicly available cross-encoder checkpoint.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{E. Did you use human annotators?}",
          "+No. All experiments use existing annotated benchmarks.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{F. Did you use AI assistants?}",
          "+Yes. AI assistants were used for code development and paper drafting. All scientific claims, experimental design, and analysis are the responsibility of the authors."

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01U8tT8ht36dK38iYWk8AEKi`</summary>

```text
15	
16	\paragraph{Metrics.}
17	We report five metrics. \textbf{Ign-F1} is the primary DocRED metric that ignores triples appearing in the training set, matching the official leaderboard protocol. \textbf{Rel-F1} measures relation extraction F1 on all triples. \textbf{Evi-F1 (TP-only)} measures evidence sentence F1 conditioned on correctly predicted triples, reflecting evidence quality independent of extraction errors. \textbf{Evi-F1 (joint)} includes false-positive and false-negative triples in the denominator following the DREEAM protocol~\citep{ma2023dreeam}, enabling cross-method evidence comparison. \textbf{EDCR} (Evidence-Decoupling Ratio) is our proposed diagnostic: the ratio of evidence recall on correctly-predicted triples to overall extraction recall, where values substantially exceeding the SFT baseline indicate RL-induced decoupling. All primary results use the development set; test set submission is pending.
18	
19	
20	\subsection{Main Results}
21	\label{sec:main_results}
22	
23	Table~\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison between SFT, no-evidence SFT, and RSFT CED on Qwen3-4B. The SFT baseline achieves 39.74 Ign-F1 with joint evidence generation. Removing evidence from the output format entirely (no-evidence SFT) improves extraction to 47.21 Ign-F1, a 7.47-point gap that quantifies the \emph{evidence tax} at the 4B scale. RSFT CED achieves a four-seed mean of 44.66 Ign-F1 (+4.92 over SFT, standard deviation 0.49) while maintaining evidence quality (Evi-F1 81.15 vs.\ 79.86 for SFT). The EDCR decreases from 0.6982 to 0.6503, confirming tighter coupling between extraction and evidence capabilities. Per-seed results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:ablation_full}.
24	
25	The remaining gap to the no-evidence upper bound (2.09 points) represents the residual cost of producing structured evidence, a cost that shrinks at larger scales as we show in Section~\ref{sec:scaling}.
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\caption{Main results on DocRED dev set (Qwen3-4B). RSFT reports four-seed mean $\pm$ std. Best Ign-F1 in \textbf{bold}; best among evidence-generating methods \underline{underlined}. Per-seed breakdown in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:ablation_full}.}
```

</details>

        "lines": [
          " Different RL algorithms produce qualitatively different failure modes when applied to joint evidence-and-extraction generation. We organize these outcomes along a \\emph{decoupling spectrum} ranging from catastrophic collapse to mild perturbation, shown in Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum} (visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:spectrum}, Appendix).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{GRPO QLoRA: catastrophic collapse.}",
          "-Under QLoRA quantization ($G{=}4$), GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 0.0138 (near zero extraction) while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 and EDCR reaches 0.9876. The model learns to produce well-formed evidence sentences but loses the ability to predict correct relation triples. This extreme decoupling co-occurs with 4-bit quantization, which constrains the effective parameter space. We hypothesize that reduced precision limits the model's ability to jointly optimize both sub-tasks, though the bf16 result below shows that GRPO with sufficient precision avoids this failure mode.",
          "+Under QLoRA quantization ($G{=}4$), GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 1.38 (near zero extraction) while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 and EDCR reaches 0.9876. The model learns to produce well-formed evidence sentences but loses the ability to predict correct relation triples. This extreme decoupling co-occurs with 4-bit quantization, which constrains the effective parameter space. We hypothesize that reduced precision limits the model's ability to jointly optimize both sub-tasks, though the bf16 result below shows that GRPO with sufficient precision avoids this failure mode.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{GRPO bf16: mild, variable.}",
          " Full-precision GRPO ($G{=}8$) avoids catastrophe. Its training trajectory shows non-monotonic Rel-F1 fluctuation (0.434 at step 30, 0.428 at step 50, 0.434 at step 70, 0.455 at step 100), stabilizing near the SFT baseline. The best checkpoint achieves Rel-F1 0.4549 with Evi-F1 80.86 and EDCR 0.6318, below the SFT baseline EDCR of 0.6982. This indicates that GRPO with sufficient precision does not exhibit the decoupling pattern, and both sub-tasks can improve jointly, though the variable trajectory suggests fragile optimization dynamics that require careful checkpoint selection."
        ]
      }
    ],

By selecting high-quality complete outputs (jointly good extraction and evidence) rather than applying gradient-based reward maximization, RSFT maintains both capabilities simultaneously (mean Rel-F1 0.4563, Evi-F1 81.15, EDCR 0.6503). The key insight is that rejection sampling operates on discrete outputs rather than differentiable reward signals, so it cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find.",

This extreme decoupling co-occurs with 4-bit quantization, which constrains the effective parameter space. We hypothesize that reduced precision limits the model's ability to jointly optimize both sub-tasks, though the bf16 result below shows that GRPO with sufficient precision avoids this failure mode.",

          " ",

          " DPO preference learning shows a progressive pattern. At 1 epoch, Rel-F1 remains at 0.4034 with Evi-F1 80.88 and EDCR 0.7139. ```text
117	\subsection{Evidence Strategy Comparison}
118	\label{sec:evidence_strategy}
119	
120	We compare joint generation against post-hoc retrieval, using Evi-F1 (joint) with the DREEAM-protocol denominator~\citep{ma2023dreeam} for fair cross-strategy comparison. Post-hoc BM25 retrieval (entity-constrained, $k{=}2$ sentences per triple) achieves higher Rel-F1 (0.4876) by avoiding the evidence tax, but produces lower Evi-F1 (joint) of 0.347 with EDCR 0.766. RSFT CED (s43) reaches Evi-F1 (joint) 0.361 with EDCR 0.654, a 1.4-point evidence advantage and 0.112 EDCR reduction. CED reranking at inference time (selecting the best of $N{=}8$ outputs by CED score) achieves Rel-F1 0.4864 and Evi-F1 81.88 (TP-only), approaching no-evidence extraction quality while preserving evidence. Joint training produces more faithful evidence grounded in the extraction reasoning rather than retrieved post-hoc; inference-time reranking complements this by recovering extraction accuracy.
121	
122	
123	\subsection{Generalization}
124	\label{sec:generalization}
125	
126	Cross-architecture validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms the scaling patterns: the evidence tax at 8B is near zero ($-0.68$ points), and RSFT CED yields +1.74 Ign-F1 gain. The RSFT improvement transfers consistently across datasets: on Re-DocRED~\citep{tan2022redocred}, RSFT achieves 41.45 Ign-F1 (+4.86pp over SFT baseline 36.59), closely matching the +4.92pp gain on DocRED. Evidence quality remains stable (Evi-F1 81.82 vs.\ 81.70 for SFT) with improved coupling (EDCR 0.6210 vs.\ 0.6674). Our best joint Evi-F1 (0.36, DREEAM-protocol denominator) falls below supervised DREEAM (54.01\%~\citep{ma2023dreeam}), a gap driven by extraction performance rather than evidence quality (TP-only Evi-F1 exceeds 80\%). Full results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:generalization_full}.
127	
```

Joint training produces more faithful evidence grounded in the extraction reasoning rather than retrieved post-hoc; inference-time reranking complements this by recovering extraction accuracy.",

RSFT CED (s43) reaches Evi-F1 (joint) 36.1 with EDCR 0.654, a 1.4-point evidence advantage and 0.112 EDCR reduction. CED reranking at inference time (selecting the best of $N{=}8$ outputs by CED score) achieves Rel-F1 48.64 and Evi-F1 81.88 (TP-only), approaching no-evidence extraction quality while preserving evidence.     "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{EDCR as a diagnostic.}\nEDCR (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}) correlates strongly with extraction quality at the method level: Spearman $\\rho = -0.965$ between EDCR and Rel-F1 (Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum}), with GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876, SFT at 0.6982, and RSFT at 0.6503. At the per-relation level, EDCR correlates with Rel-F1 ($\\rho = -0.793$) but is independent of Evi-F1 ($\\rho = -0.064$), confirming extraction-evidence decoupling across relation types (Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). RSFT avoids decoupling because discrete output selection prevents trading extraction for evidence quality (Appendix~\\ref{sec:rsft_theory}). We provide qualitative examples of these phenomena in Appendix~\\ref{sec:qualitative}.\n\n\\paragraph{CED granularity.}\nCED operates effectively at the response level for both RSFT selection and inference-time reranking, but fails at sentence-level granularity: filtering individual evidence sentences by NLI score reduces Evi-F1 by 7.7pp (Recall $-$20pp), as it disrupts co-reference chains across evidence sentences. This suggests that evidence quality is a holistic response property rather than a per-sentence attribute.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation.}\nCED and flat-NLI produce comparable performance (Rel-F1 0.4563 vs.\\ 0.4561); stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ by 1.02 Ign-F1 despite 2.96$\\times$ fewer examples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nOur findings suggest three guidelines for practitioners seeking evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation tasks where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity; (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both high extraction accuracy and evidence quality.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XtEdCmB2NHE9CvkGUf5wsR`</summary>

```text
44	
45	The document-level CED score aggregates over all predicted triples, weighted by extraction correctness: triples that match ground-truth relations contribute their CED scores, while incorrect triples receive zero credit. This coupling ensures that CED rewards both faithful evidence and accurate extraction jointly.
46	
47	We also evaluate a \emph{flat-NLI} baseline that uses only the positive entailment score $p^+$ without the contrastive term. In our experiments (Section~\ref{sec:spectrum}), CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (mean Rel-F1 difference of 0.02pp across available seeds), and both outperform random selection and no-scoring baselines. Experiments show that CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (Section~\ref{sec:analysis}), confirming that the contrastive formulation is not essential for data selection quality. We adopt CED as the default because its per-triple discrimination scores support interpretable error analysis.
48	
49	
50	\subsection{Evidence-Decoupling Ratio}
51	\label{sec:edcr}
52	
53	We introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric that quantifies the degree of misalignment between a model's extraction and evidence capabilities. EDCR is not a training component; it serves purely as an analytical tool for comparing optimization methods.
54	
55	EDCR is defined as:
56	\begin{equation}
57	\text{EDCR} = \frac{\text{Evi-Recall}_{\text{TP}}}{\text{Rel-Recall}}
58	\label{eq:edcr}
59	\end{equation}
60	where $\text{Evi-Recall}_{\text{TP}}$ is evidence sentence recall computed only over correctly predicted triples (true positives), and $\text{Rel-Recall}$ is overall relation extraction recall. The numerator captures evidence quality on the subset of triples the model gets right; the denominator captures how many triples the model identifies in total.
61	
62	The intuition is as follows. A model that correctly identifies a few triples and provides excellent evidence for them, but misses most triples entirely, will have high $\text{Evi-Recall}_{\text{TP}}$ and low $\text{Rel-Recall}$, yielding a high EDCR. This pattern signals that evidence quality has decoupled from extraction capability: the model produces faithful evidence only within a narrow comfort zone.
63	
64	EDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across optimization paradigms reveals a negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.965$). Because EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction, this correlation should be interpreted as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent discovery; its primary value lies in per-relation analysis (below). At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals variation that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\rho{=}{-}0.064$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6982) reflects the inherent cost of evidence generation; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6503) reflect improved co-optimization.
65

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Task: Evidence-Grounded Document-Level Relation Extraction}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a document $D = \\{s_1, \\ldots, s_n\\}$ consisting of $n$ sentences and a set of entities $\\mathcal{E}$ identified within $D$, the task of evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires predicting, for each entity pair $(e_h, e_t) \\in \\mathcal{E} \\times \\mathcal{E}$, a relation $r \\in \\mathcal{R} \\cup \\{\\texttt{NA}\\}$ together with a set of supporting evidence sentences $E \\subseteq D$. Standard DocRE predicts only the triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$; the evidence-grounded formulation also requires $E$, which grounds each prediction in specific textual support.\n\nIn our LLM-based approach, the model generates structured outputs that jointly encode both components. For each predicted relation, the output follows the format \\texttt{(head, relation, tail) | Evidence: [S$i$][S$j$]}, requiring the model to produce complete evidence references inline with each triple rather than as a separate retrieval step. This design contrasts with encoder-based evidence methods such as SAIS~\\citep{xiao2022sais} and DREEAM~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}, which frame evidence identification as sentence-level binary classification. Because classification over a fixed sentence set does not increase output length, these methods incur no generation overhead. Our LLM formulation requires additional tokens for each evidence reference, creating a generation burden that scales with the number of predicted triples and their evidence sentences. This scale-dependent cost is the central phenomenon we investigate.\n\n\n\\subsection{Supervised Fine-Tuning Baseline}\n\\label{sec:sft}\n\nWe fine-tune all models with LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} at rank $r{=}64$ and scaling factor $\\alpha{=}128$, applied to attention projection matrices. The training data consists of DocRED~\\citep{yao2019docred} training documents converted to the structured output format described above, where each example maps a document and its entity set to the complete set of relation triples with evidence annotations. We also train a \\emph{no-evidence} variant under identical configuration but with evidence references removed from the output format, producing only \\texttt{(head, relation, tail)}. This variant serves as an upper bound on extraction performance by eliminating the generation cost of evidence, and allows direct measurement of the evidence tax at each model scale. Detailed hyperparameters (learning rate, batch size, number of epochs) are reported in the Appendix.\n\n\n\\subsection{Optimization Paradigms}\n\\label{sec:paradigms}\n\nWe evaluate three post-SFT optimization paradigms that differ in how they incorporate quality signals from the CED scoring function (\\S\\ref{sec:ced}).\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO.}\nGroup Relative Policy Optimization~\\citep{shao2024deepseekmath} is an online RL method that eliminates the need for a separate value network by computing advantages within groups of sampled outputs. For each training document, the policy generates $G$ candidate responses, which are scored by CED. The advantage of each response is computed relative to the group mean, and the policy is updated to increase the probability of above-average outputs. We test two configurations: QLoRA with $G{=}4$ groups under 4-bit quantization, and bf16 full-precision LoRA with $G{=}8$. Because GRPO applies gradient updates that directly optimize the reward signal, evidence quality and extraction accuracy compete for gradient capacity. We analyze this multi-objective tension in Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}.\n\n\\paragraph{DPO.}\nDirect Preference Optimization~\\citep{rafailov2023direct} reformulates RLHF as a classification problem over preference pairs, without explicit reward models. We construct preference data from the SFT model's sampled outputs: for each document, the response with the highest CED score is labeled as preferred and the lowest-scoring response as rejected. DPO then adjusts the policy to increase the log-probability ratio between preferred and rejected completions. We train for 1 and 3 epochs to observe the temporal dynamics of evidence-extraction trade-offs under implicit optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{RSFT.}\nRejection Sampling Fine-Tuning is a single-round variant of iterative self-improvement methods such as STaR~\\citep{zelikman2022star}. The procedure is straightforward: the SFT model generates $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per training document, each candidate is scored by CED, and only the top-$k{=}1$ response is retained as training data. From 24,216 candidate outputs, this selection yields 9,042 training examples (37.3\\% acceptance rate). Unlike GRPO and DPO, RSFT does not apply gradient-based optimization pressure toward a reward signal. Instead, it improves training data quality through filtering: the model trains on its own best outputs rather than all outputs. This distinction is critical because discrete selection over complete outputs cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find. The contribution of RSFT in our setting is not the framework itself, which is well-established, but its application to evidence-grounded DocRE with CED as a domain-specific quality filter.\n\n\n\\subsection{Contrastive Evidence Discrimination}\n\\label{sec:ced}\n\nCED is a scoring function that evaluates the faithfulness of predicted evidence sentences to their associated relation triples. It builds on Natural Language Inference (NLI) to measure whether evidence actually entails the predicted relation, rather than merely co-occurring with the relevant entities.\n\nFor a predicted triple $(e_h, r, e_t)$ with evidence sentences $E$, CED constructs a positive NLI pair: the premise is the concatenation of sentences in $E$, and the hypothesis is a natural-language verbalization of the relation (e.g., ``$e_h$ has relation $r$ with $e_t$''). An NLI model (\\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}) produces an entailment probability $p^+$ for this pair. The contrastive component introduces hard negatives drawn from the same document. For each triple, we collect sentences that mention $e_h$ or $e_t$ but are not in the predicted evidence set. These entity-sharing distractors serve as negative premises, and the NLI model scores each to obtain $p^-_1, \\ldots, p^-_m$. The CED score for a single triple is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\text{CED}(e_h, r, e_t, E) = p^+ - \\max_j \\, p^-_j\n\\label{eq:ced}\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation forces the scoring function to distinguish sentences that genuinely support the relation from sentences that merely mention the same entities. A high CED score indicates that the evidence provides relation-specific support beyond entity co-occurrence.\n\nThe document-level CED score aggregates over all predicted triples, weighted by extraction correctness: triples that match ground-truth relations contribute their CED scores, while incorrect triples receive zero credit. This coupling ensures that CED rewards both faithful evidence and accurate extraction jointly.\n\nWe also evaluate a \\emph{flat-NLI} baseline that uses only the positive entailment score $p^+$ without the contrastive term. In our experiments (Section~\\ref{sec:spectrum}), CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (mean Rel-F1 difference of 0.02pp across available seeds), and both outperform random selection and no-scoring baselines. Experiments show that CED and flat-NLI produce comparable RSFT outcomes (Section~\\ref{sec:analysis}), confirming that the contrastive formulation is not essential for data selection quality. We adopt CED as the default because its per-triple discrimination scores support interpretable error analysis.\n\n\n\\subsection{Evidence-Decoupling Ratio}\n\\label{sec:edcr}\n\nWe introduce the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic metric that quantifies the degree of misalignment between a model's extraction and evidence capabilities. The numerator captures evidence quality on the subset of triples the model gets right; the denominator captures how many triples the model identifies in total.\n\nThe intuition is as follows. This pattern signals that evidence quality has decoupled from extraction capability: the model produces faithful evidence only within a narrow comfort zone.\n\nEDCR operates at two diagnostic levels. At the method level, comparing EDCR across optimization paradigms reveals a negative correlation with Rel-F1 (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.965$). Because EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction, this correlation should be interpreted as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent discovery; its primary value lies in per-relation analysis (below). At the per-relation level, computing EDCR for individual relation types reveals variation that is independent of Evi-F1 ($\\rho{=}{-}0.064$, $p{=}0.58$). This independence means EDCR captures relation-specific extraction-evidence misalignment that aggregate evidence metrics miss. The SFT baseline EDCR (0.6982) reflects the inherent cost of evidence generation; EDCR values substantially exceeding this level (e.g., GRPO QLoRA at 0.9876) indicate RL-induced decoupling beyond what evidence production alone explains, while values below the baseline (e.g., RSFT at 0.6503) reflect improved co-optimization.\n",

### Tool result

_source line 3436 · 2026-05-18T07:12:54.001Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BA4f59FgUEbZSmhg4eqtnU`</summary>

```text
42	Table~\ref{tab:ablation_full} reports the complete RSFT ablation across scoring functions, top-$k$ values, and random seeds. RSFT CED $k{=}1$ achieves a mean Ign-F1 of 44.66 with low variance across four seeds (standard deviation 0.49), confirming the robustness of the improvement over the SFT baseline (39.74). Increasing $k$ to 3 slightly degrades performance (43.64 Ign-F1), consistent with the intuition that stricter selection yields higher-quality training data. The flat-NLI scoring variant produces comparable Rel-F1 (mean 0.4561 vs.\ 0.4563 for CED, a difference of 0.02pp). Both CED and flat-NLI improve substantially over the SFT baseline while maintaining or improving Evi-F1, confirming that the RSFT gains are driven by quality-based data selection rather than by the specific contrastive formulation.
43	
44	\paragraph{NLI scoring comparison.} Across all available seeds, CED RSFT achieves mean Rel-F1 of 0.4563 (seeds 42, 43, 44, 45: range 0.4514--0.4621), and flat-NLI RSFT achieves mean Rel-F1 of 0.4561 (seeds 42, 43: range 0.4535--0.4586). The 0.02pp difference suggests comparable performance: CED's contrastive design does not substantially outperform simpler NLI scoring for RSFT data selection. We adopt CED as the default because its contrastive formulation produces per-triple discrimination scores that support interpretable error analysis.
45	
46	\paragraph{Top-$k$ selection.} Best-of-8 ($k{=}1$) yields mean Ign-F1 44.66 and Rel-F1 0.4563, while top-3-of-8 ($k{=}3$) produces 43.64 and 0.4454. The selected training data confirms the quality gap: $k{=}1$ data has mean extraction F1 of 0.6157 and mean CED of 0.6683, versus 0.5761 and 0.6205 for $k{=}3$. Despite $k{=}3$ providing 2.96$\times$ more training examples, the additional lower-ranked samples introduce noise that degrades final performance.
47	
```

Its SFT baseline achieves 53.84 Ign-F1 (Rel-F1 55.14, Evi-F1 81.51), and the no-evidence variant reaches 54.52 Ign-F1 (Rel-F1 56.24). The evidence tax of $-0.68$ points mirrors the near-zero tax seen for Qwen3-8B ($-0.28$), supporting our claim that 8B-class models absorb the cost of joint evidence generation regardless of architecture family. RSFT CED improves LLaMA to 55.58 Ign-F1 (Rel-F1 0.5674, Evi-F1 82.14, EDCR 0.6128), a +1.74 point gain consistent with the pattern at the 8B scale (Qwen3-8B: +3.82). A second seed (s43: 56.19 Ign-F1, EDCR 0.6073) confirms robustness.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Cross-dataset robustness.}",
          " On Re-DocRED~\\citep{tan2022redocred}, the SFT baseline achieves 36.59 Ign-F1 (Evi-F1 81.70, EDCR 0.6674). RSFT CED improves this to 41.45 Ign-F1 (Evi-F1 81.82, EDCR 0.6210), a +4.86pp gain that closely matches the +4.92pp improvement on DocRED. This consistency across datasets with different annotation protocols confirms that RSFT's benefit generalizes beyond training-set-specific artifacts. Evidence quality remains stable across datasets (Evi-F1 81.82 vs.\\ 81.15 on DocRED), and the EDCR reduction ($-0.046$) mirrors the DocRED pattern, further supporting the robustness of CED-based selection."
        ]
      }
    ],

```text
150:\caption{Training dynamics of GRPO under different configurations. (a)~Relation F1 across checkpoints: G=4 QLoRA suffers catastrophic collapse at step~10, while G=8 bf16 remains stable. (b)~Evidence F1 stays intact for G=8 bf16, confirming the generation mechanism itself does not fail. (c)~Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) reveals near-total decoupling in G=4 QLoRA (extraction collapses to Rel-F1 0.014), while G=8 bf16 maintains healthy ratios.}
